The barista manages everything from taking orders to making coffee and cooking with local produce.       "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffeehouse",
      "title": "Coffeehouse - Wikipedia",
      "content": "In Italy, cafés are similar to those found in France and known as bar. They typically serve a variety of espresso coffee, cakes and alcoholic drinks. Bars in city centers usually have different prices for consumption at the bar and consumption at a table.\n\n#### Ireland\n\n[edit]\n\nToday, the word café – also spelled cafe, but always pronounced as two syllables – is used for most coffeehouses. It has also come to be used for a type of diner that offers cooked meals (again, without alcoholic beverages) which can be standalone or operating within shopping centres or department stores.\n\n#### United Kingdom\n\n[edit] [...] . In continental Europe, some cafés even serve alcoholic drinks, and in West Asia, coffeehouses may offer a flavored tobacco smoked through a hookah, called shisha in most varieties of Arabic or nargile in Levantine Arabic, Greek, and Turkish.\n\nWhile café may mean a coffeehouse, it tends to have a different meaning \"Cafe (British)\") in Britain: a diner or \"greasy spoon\"; it is also used for a teahouse or other casual eating and drinking place. A coffeehouse may resemble a bar or restaurant, but differs from a cafeteria (a canteen, a restaurant without table service). Coffeehouse operation ranges from management of an independent venue by its owner to franchises of a large multinational corporation. [...] ### Europe\n\n[edit]\n\n as well as indoors. Some, particularly in Southern Europe, also serve alcoholic drinks (e.g., wine). In the Netherlands and Belgium, a café is the equivalent of a bar, and also sells alcoholic drinks. In the Netherlands a koffiehuis serves coffee, while a \"coffee shop\" (the English term) sells \"soft\" drugs (cannabis \"Cannabis (drug)\") and hashish) and is generally not allowed to sell alcoholic drinks. In France, most cafés serve as lunch restaurants in the day, and bars in the evening. They generally do not have pastries except in the mornings, when a croissant or pain au chocolat can be bought with breakfast coffee.[citation needed]",
      "score": 0.20051105,

version](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&printable=yes \"Printable version of this page [p]\")\n\nIn other projects\n\n* [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9)\n* [Wikidata item](https://www.wikidata.org/wiki/Special:EntityPage/Q30022 \"Structured data on this page hosted by Wikidata [g]\")\n\nAppearance\n\nmove to sidebar hide\n\nFrom Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia\n\nEstablishment that serves coffee\n\n \n\n\"Cafe\" redirects here; not to be confused with [Cafeteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafeteria \"Cafeteria\") or [Cafe (British)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafe_(British) \"Cafe (British)\").\n\nFor other uses, see [Cafe (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafe_(disambiguation) \"Cafe (disambiguation)\") and [Coffeehouse (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffeehouse_(disambiguation) \"Coffeehouse (disambiguation)\").\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Caf%C3%A9_de_Flore.jpg/250px-Caf%C3%A9_de_Flore.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Café_de_Flore.jpg)\n\nThe [Café de Flore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_de_Flore \"Café de Flore\") on the [Rive Gauche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rive_Gauche \"Rive Gauche\") in Paris is one of the oldest coffeehouses in the city. Its famous clientele included writers and philosophers.\n\nA **coffeehouse**, **coffee shop**, or **café**, is an establishment that serves various types of [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee \"Coffee\") drinks like [espresso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espresso \"Espresso\"), [latte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latte \"Latte\"), [americano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffè_americano \"Caffè americano\") and [cappuccino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappuccino \"Cappuccino\"), as well as other beverages. An [espresso bar](#Espresso_bar) specializes in serving espresso and espresso-based drinks. Some coffeehouses may serve [iced coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iced_coffee \"Iced coffee\") among other cold drinks, as well as non-caffeinated drinks. A coffeehouse may also serve food, such as light snacks, [sandwiches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandwich \"Sandwich\"), [muffins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muffin \"Muffin\"), [cakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cake \"Cake\"), [breads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread \"Bread\"), [pastries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastries \"Pastries\") or [doughnuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doughnut \"Doughnut\"). Many [doughnut shops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doughnut_shop \"Doughnut shop\") in Canada and the U.S. serve coffee to accompany doughnuts, so these can also be classified as coffee shops, although doughnut shops tend to be more casual and serve cheaper fare (suiting [take-out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Take-out \"Take-out\") and [drive-through](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-through \"Drive-through\"), popular in those countries).[[1]](#cite_note-1)[[2]](#cite_note-2) In continental Europe, some cafés even serve alcoholic drinks, and in West Asia, coffeehouses may offer a flavored tobacco smoked through a [hookah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hookah \"Hookah\"), called *shisha* in most varieties of [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic \"Arabic\") or *nargile* in [Levantine Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic \"Levantine Arabic\"), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language \"Greek language\"), and [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language \"Turkish language\").\n\nWhile *café* may mean a coffeehouse, it tends to have [a different meaning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafe_(British) \"Cafe (British)\") in Britain:[[3]](#cite_note-3) a [diner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diner \"Diner\") or \"[greasy spoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greasy_spoon \"Greasy spoon\")\"; it is also used for a [teahouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teahouse \"Teahouse\") or other casual eating and drinking place.[[4]](#cite_note-4)[[5]](#cite_note-5)[[6]](#cite_note-6) A coffeehouse may resemble a bar or restaurant, but differs from a [cafeteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafeteria \"Cafeteria\") (a canteen, a restaurant without table service). Coffeehouse operation ranges from management of an independent venue by its owner to franchises of a large [multinational corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_corporation \"Multinational corporation\").\n\nFrom a cultural standpoint, a coffeehouse largely serves as a center of social interaction: it provides patrons with a place to meet, talk, read, write, entertain one another, or pass the time, whether individually or in small groups. A coffeehouse can serve as an informal [social club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_club \"Social club\") for its regular members.[[7]](#cite_note-7)[*[better source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")*] From as early as the 1950s [Beatnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatnik \"Beatnik\") era and the 1960s [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music \"Folk music\") scene, coffeehouses have hosted singer–songwriter performances, typically in the evening.[[8]](#cite_note-Groce_Coffeehouses-8) The digital age saw the rise of the [Internet café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_café \"Internet café\") along similar principles.[*[vague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Vagueness \"Wikipedia:Vagueness\")*]\n\n## Etymology\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=1 \"Edit section: Etymology\")]\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| **This section has multiple issues.** Please help **[improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse \"Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse\")** or discuss these issues on the **[talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Coffeehouse \"Talk:Coffeehouse\")**. *([Learn how and when to remove these messages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |  | | --- | | The examples and perspectives in this section **may not represent a [worldwide view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:WikiProject_Countering_systemic_bias \"Wikipedia:WikiProject Countering systemic bias\") of the subject**. The specific issue is: **Overwhelmingly Eurocentric; focuses on French/Italian/English while Arabic origin is treated as a brief footnote. Coffee originated in Arabia.** You may [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse \"Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse\"), discuss the issue on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Coffeehouse \"Talk:Coffeehouse\"), or create a new section, as appropriate. *(May 2026)* *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |  |  | | --- | | This section **is missing information** about the full etymology from Arabic *qahwa* through Ottoman Turkish to European languages, and the non-European history of coffeehouses. Please expand the section to include this information. Further details may exist on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Coffeehouse# \"Talk:Coffeehouse\"). *(May 2026)* |  |  | | --- | | This section **may be [unbalanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Neutral_point_of_view#Undue_weight \"Wikipedia:Neutral point of view\") towards certain viewpoints**. Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit) by adding information on neglected viewpoints. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Coffeehouse \"Talk:Coffeehouse\"). *(May 2026)* |  *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Evolution_of_the_word_Coffee.svg/250px-Evolution_of_the_word_Coffee.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Evolution_of_the_word_Coffee.svg)\n\nThe word for coffee in various European languages[[9]](#cite_note-9)\n\nThe Arabic word *qahwa* (قهوة) is the origin of the Italian *caffè* (first attested as *caveé* in [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice \"Venice\") in 1570[[10]](#cite_note-10)), from which the English *coffee* and French *café* both derive.[[11]](#cite_note-auto-11)[[12]](#cite_note-12) The Arabic term originally meant a type of wine, but after the wine ban by [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam \"Islam\"), the name was transferred to coffee, thought to have a similar rousing effect.[[13]](#cite_note-13) European knowledge of coffee (the plant, its seeds, and the drink made from the seeds) came through European contact with [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey \"Turkey\"), likely via Venetian–[Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\") trade relations.\n\n*Café* (French: [[kafe]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/French \"Help:IPA/French\") [ⓘ](/wiki/File:LL-Q150_(fra)-GrandCelinien-caf%C3%A9.wav \"File:LL-Q150 (fra)-GrandCelinien-café.wav\")) is the French word for both *coffee* and *coffeehouse;*[[11]](#cite_note-auto-11)[[14]](#cite_note-14) it was adopted (as /ˈkæfeɪ/) by English-speaking countries in the late 19th century.[[15]](#cite_note-15) The Italian spelling, *caffè*, is also sometimes used in English.[[16]](#cite_note-16) In [Southern England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_England \"Southern England\"), especially around London in the 1950s, the French pronunciation was often facetiously altered to [/kæf/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English \"Help:IPA/English\") and spelt *caff*.[[17]](#cite_note-17)[[a]](#cite_note-19)\n\nThe English word *café* to describe a *place* that serves coffee and snacks (rather than the drink itself) is derived from the French *café.* The first café in France is believed to have opened in 1660.[[11]](#cite_note-auto-11) Meanwhile, the first café in Europe is believed to have been opened in [Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade \"Belgrade\"), [Ottoman Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Serbia \"Ottoman Serbia\") in 1522 as a [Kafana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafana \"Kafana\") (Serbian coffeehouse).[[19]](#cite_note-20)\n\nThe [translingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translingual \"Translingual\") word root /kafe/ appears in many European languages with various naturalized spellings, including [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language \"Portuguese language\") and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language \"French language\") (*café*); [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language \"German language\") (*Kaffeehaus* or *Café*); [Swedish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_language \"Swedish language\") (*kafé* or *fik*); [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language \"Finnish language\") (*kahvila*); [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language \"Spanish language\") (*cafetería*); [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language \"Italian language\") (*caffè* or *caffetteria*); [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language \"Polish language\") (*kawa*); [Serbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_language \"Serbian language\") (*кафа / kafa*); [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language \"Ukrainian language\") (*кава* (kava)); [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language \"Turkish language\") (*kahvehane).*\n\n## Early history\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=2 \"Edit section: Early history\")]\n\nMain article: [Coffeehouses in Arabic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffeehouses_in_Arabic_culture \"Coffeehouses in Arabic culture\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Palestinian_women_grinding_coffee_beans.jpg/250px-Palestinian_women_grinding_coffee_beans.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palestinian_women_grinding_coffee_beans.jpg)\n\nPalestinian women grinding coffee beans\n\nEarly forms of the beverage now known as coffee were consumed in Yemen, where [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism \"Sufism\") orders used [*qahwa*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qahwah \"Qahwah\") to maintain wakefulness during nighttime *[dhikr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhikr \"Dhikr\")* recitations.[[20]](#cite_note-21)[[21]](#cite_note-:10-22) This early coffee-based beverage was named after the port city of [Mokha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mokha \"Mokha\"), from which coffee beans were first exported to the other parts of the Arab world.[[22]](#cite_note-23)[[23]](#cite_note-24) Yemen was once one of the world's major producers and exporters, with \"Mocha\" coffee. Yemeni traders played a crucial role in spreading both the beverage and its associated rituals, contributing to its growing popularity across the Islamic world.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\nIn the 15th century, coffee cultivation and consumption began in Yemen, where merchants transformed it into a lucrative and commercial product. The beans, often sourced from the [Ethiopian highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Highlands \"Ethiopian Highlands\"), were roasted, ground, and brewed in a method that closely resembles modern coffee preparation. By the early 16th century, coffee spread northward to Mecca, Cairo, and Damascus, where coffeehouses — known as *maqāhī* (مقاهي) became popular meeting places.[[24]](#cite_note-Albanese2017-25)\n\n### Ottoman Empire\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=3 \"Edit section: Ottoman Empire\")]\n\nMain article: [Ottoman coffeehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_coffeehouse \"Ottoman coffeehouse\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/MeddahOttomman.png/250px-MeddahOttomman.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MeddahOttomman.png)\n\n[Ottoman miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature \"Ottoman miniature\") of a [meddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meddah \"Meddah\") performing at a coffeehouse\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Meddah_story_teller.png/250px-Meddah_story_teller.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Meddah_story_teller.png)\n\nStoryteller (*[meddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Ottoman_Empire \"Culture of the Ottoman Empire\")*) at a coffeehouse in the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\"). The first coffeehouses appeared in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world \"Muslim world\") in the 15th century.\n\nThe first coffeehouses appeared in [Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus \"Damascus\"). These coffeehouses also made their way to [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca \"Mecca\"), in the [Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula \"Arabian Peninsula\") in the 15th century, then spread to the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\")'s capital of [Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul \"Istanbul\"), when two Arab merchants, Hakem of Aleppo and Shems of Damascus, opened the first coffeehouse in the Tahtakale district in the 16th century and to [Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad \"Baghdad\").[[25]](#cite_note-Ayvazoglu-26)[[26]](#cite_note-Iranica2-27)[[27]](#cite_note-OzkaymakBas-28)[[28]](#cite_note-Karhan-29)\n\nCoffeehouses became popular meeting places where people gathered to drink coffee, have conversations, play board games such as [chess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chess \"Chess\") and [backgammon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backgammon \"Backgammon\"), listen to stories and music, and discuss news and politics. They became known as \"schools of wisdom\" for the type of clientele they attracted, and their free and frank discourse.[[29]](#cite_note-britannica-30)[[30]](#cite_note-31)\n\nCoffeehouses in Mecca became a concern of [imams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam \"Imam\"), who viewed them as places for political gatherings and drinking, leading to bans between 1512 and 1524.[[31]](#cite_note-32) However, these bans could not be maintained, as coffee became ingrained in daily ritual and culture among Arabs and neighboring peoples.[[29]](#cite_note-britannica-30) The [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\") chronicler [İbrahim Peçevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/İbrahim_Peçevi \"İbrahim Peçevi\") reports in his writings (1642–49) about the opening of the first coffeehouse (*kiva han*) in Constantinople:\n\n> Until the year 962 [1555], in the High, God-Guarded city of Constantinople, as well as in Ottoman lands generally, coffee and coffeehouses did not exist. About that year, a fellow called Hakam from [Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleppo \"Aleppo\") and a wag called Shams from Damascus came to the city; they each opened a large shop in the district called Tahtakale, and began to purvey coffee.[[32]](#cite_note-33)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/1004-CoffeeSceneCairo18th.jpg/250px-1004-CoffeeSceneCairo18th.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1004-CoffeeSceneCairo18th.jpg)\n\nA coffeehouse in [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo \"Cairo\"), 18th century\n\n### Iran\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=4 \"Edit section: Iran\")]\n\nThe 17th-century French traveler and writer [Jean Chardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Chardin \"Jean Chardin\") gave a lively description of the [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran \"Iran\") coffeehouse (*qahveh khaneh* in [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language \"Persian language\")):\n\n> People engage in conversation, for it is there that news is communicated and where those interested in politics criticize the government in all freedom and without being fearful, since the government does not heed what the people say. Innocent games ... resembling [checkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Checkers \"Checkers\"), hopscotch, and chess, are played. In addition, [mollas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah \"Mullah\"), [dervishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dervish \"Dervish\"), and poets take turns telling stories in verse or in prose. The narrations by the mollas and the dervishes are moral lessons, like our sermons, but it is not considered scandalous not to pay attention to them. No one is forced to give up his game or his conversation because of it. A molla will stand up in the middle, or at one end of the qahveh-khaneh, and begin to preach in a loud voice, or a dervish enters all of a sudden, and chastises the assembled on the vanity of the world and its material goods. It often happens that two or three people talk at the same time, one on one side, the other on the opposite, and sometimes one will be a preacher and the other a storyteller.[[33]](#cite_note-34)\n\n### Mughal Empire\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=5 \"Edit section: Mughal Empire\")]\n\nConsumption of Turkish coffee is attested to in the [Mughal court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire \"Mughal Empire\"), and appears in [Mughal art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_art \"Mughal art\") from the 16th century, as is the existence of *qahwakhanas* (coffeehouses) in [Shahjahanabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahjahanabad \"Shahjahanabad\") (Old Delhi).[[34]](#cite_note-GuptaH-35)[[35]](#cite_note-BiswasN-36)\n\n## Modern history\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=6 \"Edit section: Modern history\")]\n\n### Europe\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=7 \"Edit section: Europe\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/ParisCafeDiscussion.png/250px-ParisCafeDiscussion.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ParisCafeDiscussion.png)\n\n\"Discussing the War in a Paris Café\", *[The Illustrated London News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Illustrated_London_News \"The Illustrated London News\")*, 17 September 1870, during the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War \"Franco-Prussian War\")\n\nIn the 17th century, coffee appeared for the first time in Europe outside the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\"), and coffeehouses were established, soon becoming increasingly popular. The first public coffeehouse in Europe proper was opened in [Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade \"Belgrade\"), year 1521 and shortly after the city fell to the Ottomans. It was followed by coffeehouse in [Sarajevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarajevo \"Sarajevo\"), at then lively part of the town called [Bentbaša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bentbaša?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Bentbaša (page does not exist)\"), around year 1532. [Bentbaša](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bentbaša?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Bentbaša (page does not exist)\") was on the route of caravans and was thus a popular meeting point. Outside Ottoman Empire in Europe, first coffeehouse is said to have appeared in 1632 in [Livorno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livorno \"Livorno\"), Italy, founded by a Jewish merchant,[[36]](#cite_note-37)[[37]](#cite_note-38) or in 1640, in Venice.[[38]](#cite_note-39) In the 19th and 20th centuries in Europe, coffeehouses were very often meeting places for writers and artists.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n#### Austria\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=8 \"Edit section: Austria\")]\n\nMain article: [Viennese coffee house culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viennese_coffee_house_culture \"Viennese coffee house culture\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Cafe-schwarzenberg-innen-viennaphoto-at.jpg/250px-Cafe-schwarzenberg-innen-viennaphoto-at.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cafe-schwarzenberg-innen-viennaphoto-at.jpg)\n\nA [Viennese café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viennese_coffee_house \"Viennese coffee house\")\n\nThe traditional tale of the origins of the [Viennese café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viennese_café \"Viennese café\") begins with the mysterious sacks of green beans left behind when the Turks were defeated in the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna \"Battle of Vienna\") in 1683. All the sacks of coffee were granted to the victorious Polish king, [Jan III Sobieski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_III_Sobieski \"Jan III Sobieski\"), who in turn gave them to one of his officers, [Jerzy Franciszek Kulczycki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerzy_Franciszek_Kulczycki \"Jerzy Franciszek Kulczycki\") (also known as Georg Kolschitzky), a Ukrainian Cossack and Polish diplomat of Ruthenian descent. Kulczycki, according to the tale, then began the first coffeehouse in [Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna \"Vienna\") with the hoard, also being the first to serve coffee with milk.[[39]](#cite_note-Weinberg2002-75-40)\n\nHowever, it is now widely accepted that the first Viennese coffeehouse was actually opened by an Armenian merchant named Johannes Diodato (also known as Johannes Theodat) in 1685.[[40]](#cite_note-Teply_1980-41)[[41]](#cite_note-Weinberg2002-77-42) Fifteen years later, four other Armenians owned coffeehouses. The [culture of drinking coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_culture \"Coffee culture\") was itself widespread in the country[*[vague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Vagueness \"Wikipedia:Vagueness\")*] in the second half of the 18th century.\n\nOver time, a [Viennese coffeehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viennese_coffeehouse \"Viennese coffeehouse\") culture developed. Writers, artists, musicians, intellectuals, bon vivants and their financiers met, and new coffee varieties were served. People played cards or chess, worked, read, thought, composed, discussed, argued, observed and just chatted. Much information was also obtained, because local and foreign newspapers were freely available to all customers. This form of coffeehouse culture spread throughout the [Habsburg Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_Empire \"Habsburg Empire\") in the 19th century.[[42]](#cite_note-43)[[43]](#cite_note-44)\n\nScientific theories, political plans and artistic projects were worked out and discussed in Viennese coffeehouses all over Central Europe. [James Joyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Joyce \"James Joyce\") enjoyed his coffee in a Viennese coffeehouse on the [Adriatic Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adriatic_Sea \"Adriatic Sea\") in [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste \"Trieste\"), then and now the main port for coffee and coffee processing in Italy and Central Europe.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] From there, the Viennese *Kapuziner* coffee developed into today's [cappuccino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappuccino \"Cappuccino\"). This special multicultural atmosphere of the Habsburg coffeehouses was largely destroyed by the later Nazism and communism and today can only be found in a few places that have long been in the slipstream of history, such as Vienna or Trieste.[[44]](#cite_note-45)[[45]](#cite_note-46)[[46]](#cite_note-47)[[47]](#cite_note-48)\n\n#### England\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=9 \"Edit section: England\")]\n\nMain article: [English coffeehouses in the 17th and 18th centuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_coffeehouses_in_the_17th_and_18th_centuries \"English coffeehouses in the 17th and 18th centuries\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Inside_the_Tea_Shop_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5539583.jpg/250px-Inside_the_Tea_Shop_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5539583.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Inside_the_Tea_Shop_-_geograph.org.uk_-_5539583.jpg)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Cafe_interior_-_geograph.org.uk_-_4801991.jpg/250px-Cafe_interior_-_geograph.org.uk_-_4801991.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cafe_interior_-_geograph.org.uk_-_4801991.jpg)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/UoH_Mar24_Rebrand_New_Arts_Cafe_Wall_Graphics.jpg/250px-UoH_Mar24_Rebrand_New_Arts_Cafe_Wall_Graphics.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:UoH_Mar24_Rebrand_New_Arts_Cafe_Wall_Graphics.jpg)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Easton_Lodge_Gardens%2C_Little_Easton%2C_Essex%2C_England_outdoor_caf%C3%A9_17.jpg/250px-Easton_Lodge_Gardens%2C_Little_Easton%2C_Essex%2C_England_outdoor_caf%C3%A9_17.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Easton_Lodge_Gardens,_Little_Easton,_Essex,_England_outdoor_café_17.jpg)\n\nVarious cafés in England\n\nThe first coffeehouse in England was opened on the High Street (on the site of the later Grand Café) in [Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford \"Oxford\") in 1650[[48]](#cite_note-49) or 1651[[49]](#cite_note-CBH-50)[*[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources \"Wikipedia:Citing sources\")*] by \"Jacob the Jew\". A second, competing coffeehouse was opened across the street (on the site of the later [Queen's Lane Coffee House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen's_Lane_Coffee_House \"Queen's Lane Coffee House\")) in 1654, by \"Cirques Jobson, the Jew\".[[50]](#cite_note-51) In [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London \"London\"), the earliest coffeehouse was established by [Pasqua Rosée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasqua_Rosée \"Pasqua Rosée\") (described as Greek,[[51]](#cite_note-52)[[52]](#cite_note-53) Armenian,[[53]](#cite_note-54)[[54]](#cite_note-55) or Turkish[[55]](#cite_note-56)) in 1652.[[56]](#cite_note-57) [Anthony Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Wood_(antiquary) \"Anthony Wood (antiquary)\") observed of the coffeehouses of Oxford in 1674 \"The decay of study, and consequently of learning, are coffy houses, to which most scholars retire and spend much of the day in hearing and speaking of news, in speaking vilely of their superiors.\"[[57]](#cite_note-58) Pasqua Rosée was the servant of a trader in goods from the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\") named Daniel Edwards, who imported the coffee and assisted Rosée in setting up the establishment there.[[58]](#cite_note-Weinberg_2002-59)[[59]](#cite_note-60)\n\nFrom 1670 to 1685, London coffeehouses began to increase in number, and also in political importance due to their popularity as places of debate.[[60]](#cite_note-61) For the first several years, London's coffeehouses were the preserve of \"a well-educated and commercial elite\",[[61]](#cite_note-WhiteM-62) but from the 1660s their popularity increased. By 1675, there were more than 3,000 coffeehouses in England;[[62]](#cite_note-63) and in London alone there were perhaps 550 at their 18th-century peak.[[63]](#cite_note-64) Many men found a coffeehouse a convenient place for doing business, holding consultations there and having mail for them sent there, as well as keeping up with news. The coffeehouses were great social levelers, open to all men and indifferent to social status, and as a result associated with equality and republicanism. Entry gave access to books or print news. The rich intellectual atmosphere of early London coffeehouses was available to anyone who could pay the sometimes one penny entry fee, giving them the name \"[penny universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_coffeehouses_in_the_17th_and_18th_centuries#Early_Oxford_coffeehouses_( \"English coffeehouses in the 17th and 18th centuries\")\".[[61]](#cite_note-WhiteM-62)\n\nThough [Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England \"Charles II of England\") tried to suppress coffeehouses[[b]](#cite_note-65) as places where the \"Idle and disaffected persons\" met, and where \"divers False, Malitious and Scandalous Reports are devised and spread abroad, to the Defamation of His Majesties Government\",[[64]](#cite_note-66) the public still flocked to them. For several decades following the [Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_England \"Restoration England\"), the wits gathered around [John Dryden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dryden \"John Dryden\") at [Will's Coffee House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will's_Coffee_House \"Will's Coffee House\"), in [Bow Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_Street \"Bow Street\") (although [Jonathan Swift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Swift \"Jonathan Swift\") was unimpressed).[[61]](#cite_note-WhiteM-62) As coffeehouses were believed to be areas where anti-government gossip could easily spread, [Queen Mary II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_II \"Mary II\") and the London City magistrates tried to prosecute people who frequented coffeehouses as they were liable to \"spread false and seditious reports\". [William III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_III_of_England \"William III of England\")'s privy council also suppressed [Jacobite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobitism \"Jacobitism\") sympathizers in the 1680s and 1690s in coffeehouses as places that they believed harbored plotters against the regime.[[65]](#cite_note-67)\n\nBy the early 18th century, different coffeehouses attracted different clienteles, divided by occupation or opinion, such as [Tories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tory \"Tory\") and [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party) \"Whigs (British political party)\"), wits and [stockjobbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockjobber \"Stockjobber\"), merchants and lawyers, booksellers and authors, men of fashion or the \"cits\" of [the City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_London \"City of London\"). According to one French visitor, [Abbé Prévost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbé_Prévost \"Abbé Prévost\"), \"You read [in coffeehouses] for two-pence all the papers for or against the administration\", and they were \"the seats of English liberty\".[[66]](#cite_note-68)\n\nCoffeehouses not only boosted the popularity of print news culture, they also helped the growth of various financial markets including insurance and stocks. [Lloyd's Coffee House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd's_Coffee_House \"Lloyd's Coffee House\") was where underwriters of ship insurance met to do business, leading to the establishment of [Lloyd's of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd's_of_London \"Lloyd's of London\") insurance market and other related businesses.[[61]](#cite_note-WhiteM-62) In 1773 the stockbrokers who had been meeting at [New Jonathan's Coffee-house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan's_Coffee-House \"Jonathan's Coffee-House\") renamed it \"[The Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Stock_Exchange \"London Stock Exchange\")\".[[67]](#cite_note-69)\n\nBy the 1750s, the English consumption of tea had overtaken that of coffee. As tea could be easily prepared at home, newspapers were cheap, and there was a greater variety of places for leisure and entertainment, there was no obvious demand for publicly available teahouses. Later in the century, coffeehouses tended, via pricing and memberships, to cater for only a richer clientele, and \"the death of coffee-house culture was assured\".[[61]](#cite_note-WhiteM-62)\n\nIn [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era \"Victorian era\") England, the [temperance movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperance_movement \"Temperance movement\") set up coffeehouses (also known as coffee taverns) for the working classes, as a place of relaxation free of [alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_(drug) \"Alcohol (drug)\"), an alternative to the [public house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_house \"Public house\").[[68]](#cite_note-70)[[69]](#cite_note-71)\n\n#### Finland\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=10 \"Edit section: Finland\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Bulevardin_Ekberg_talvella_-_Marit_Henriksson.jpg/250px-Bulevardin_Ekberg_talvella_-_Marit_Henriksson.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bulevardin_Ekberg_talvella_-_Marit_Henriksson.jpg)\n\n[Café Ekberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Ekberg \"Café Ekberg\") in [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki \"Helsinki\") in 2024\n\nFinland's first coffeehouse, Kaffehus, was founded in [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku \"Turku\") in 1778.[[70]](#cite_note-72) The oldest coffeehouse still operating in [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki \"Helsinki\"), [Café Ekberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Ekberg \"Café Ekberg\"), was founded in 1852.[[71]](#cite_note-73)\n\n#### France\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=11 \"Edit section: France\")]\n\nWhen [Soliman Aga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_Aga \"Suleiman Aga\") (sent to Versailles in 1669 by Sultan [Mehmed IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_IV \"Mehmed IV\")) returned home, a member of his retinue, Pascal, remained and sold coffee from a stall at the market of Saint-Germain-des-Prés. He soon thereafter opened a coffeehouse in Paris, on the Quai de l'École near the [Pont Neuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pont_Neuf \"Pont Neuf\"), and others from the Near East rivaled him. But the fad for Turquerie soon ended.\n\n\"[I]t was not until the establishment of the [Café de Procope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Procope \"Café Procope\") in 1689 that coffee found a truly Parisian expression\".[[72]](#cite_note-74) Owned by the Sicilian Procopio Cutò (François Procope), this was a popular meeting place of the French [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment \"Age of Enlightenment\"); the *[Encyclopédie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopédie \"Encyclopédie\")* (1751–1772) of [Diderot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_Diderot \"Denis Diderot\") and [D'Alembert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_le_Rond_d'Alembert \"Jean le Rond d'Alembert\") is said to have had its start in conversations there between the two.[[73]](#cite_note-fitch43-75)\n\n> The issue of slavery had a profound effect on the pre-Revolutionary thinkers who gathered at the Café Procope in Paris, including [Montesquieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montesquieu \"Montesquieu\"), [Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau \"Jean-Jacques Rousseau\"), and Diderot. The ethics of the [slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nantes_slave_trade \"Nantes slave trade\") were discussed whilst its very fruits were consumed, a dark liquid produced by dark skins in the depths of dark despair. Diderot wrote that the trade \"is a business which violates religion, morality, natural law, and all human rights\", whilst Rousseau railed against the supposed right to enslave as \"absurd and meaningless\".[[74]](#cite_note-76)\n\nIn its decor too, Café Procope had a lasting influence. Procopio had installed wall mirrors, marble-topped tables, and more that he had had removed from a bath-house that he had bought, thereby establishing what has become a convention for many other European cafés.[[73]](#cite_note-fitch43-75)\n\n#### Hungary\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=12 \"Edit section: Hungary\")]\n\nThe first known cafés in [Pest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest \"Budapest\") date back to 1714 when a house intended to serve as a café (Balázs Kávéfőző) was purchased. Minutes of the Pest City Council from 1729 mention complaints by the Balázs café and Franz Reschfellner café against the Italian-originated café of Francesco Bellieno for selling underpriced coffee.[[75]](#cite_note-77)\n\n#### Ireland\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=13 \"Edit section: Ireland\")]\n\nIn the 18th century, [Dublin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dublin \"Dublin\") coffeehouses functioned as early reading centers and the emergence of circulation and subscription libraries that provided greater access to printed material for the public.[*[vague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Vagueness \"Wikipedia:Vagueness\")*] The connection of the coffeehouse with virtually every aspect of the print trade was evidenced by the incorporation of printing, publishing, selling, and viewing of newspapers, pamphlets and books on the premises, most notably for [Dick's Coffee House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick's_Coffee_House \"Dick's Coffee House\"), owned by [Richard Pue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Pue \"Richard Pue\"); thus contributing to a culture of reading and increased literacy.[[76]](#cite_note-Abbas_2014-78) These coffeehouses were social magnets where different strata of society joined to discuss topics covered by the newspapers and pamphlets. Most coffeehouses of the 18th century would eventually be equipped with their own printing presses or incorporate a bookshop.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n#### Italy\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=14 \"Edit section: Italy\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Entrata_Caff%C3%A8_Florian.jpg/250px-Entrata_Caff%C3%A8_Florian.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Entrata_Caffè_Florian.jpg)\n\n[Caffè Florian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffè_Florian \"Caffè Florian\") in [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice \"Venice\")\n\nThe first café in Venice opened in 1683 under the [Procuratie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Procuratie \"Procuratie\") Nuove of [Piazza San Marco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piazza_San_Marco \"Piazza San Marco\").[[77]](#cite_note-79) The concept was popular:\n\n> On [Piazza San Marco] alone in the eighteenth century there were, under the Procuratie Vecchie, the following cafés: the *Re di Francia*, the *Abbondanza*, *Pitt l'eroe*, the *Regina d'Ungheria*, the *Orfeo*, the *Redentore*, the *Coraggio*, the *Speranza*, the *Arco Celeste* and *[Quadri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffè_Quadri \"Caffè Quadri\")* opened in 1775 by Giorgio Quadri of [Corfù](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corfu \"Corfu\"), who was the first to serve genuine [Turkish coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_coffee \"Turkish coffee\"). Under the Procuratie Nuove were the *Angelo Custode*, the *Duca di Toscana*, the *Buon genio*, the *Doge*, the *Imperatore*, the *Imperatrice della Russia*, the *Tamerlano*, the *Fontana di Diana*, the *Dame Venete*, the *Aurora*, the *Piante d'oro*, the *Arabo*, the *Piastrelle*, the *Pace*, the *Venezia trionfante*, and *[Florian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffè_Florian \"Caffè Florian\")*, opened in 1720 by Floriano Francesconi.[[78]](#cite_note-80)\n\nDuring the 18th century, the oldest extant coffeehouses in Italy were established. Venice aside, these included [Antico Caffè Greco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antico_Caffè_Greco \"Antico Caffè Greco\") (circa 1760) in [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome \"Rome\"),[[79]](#cite_note-81) [Caffè Pedrocchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedrocchi_Café \"Pedrocchi Café\") in [Padua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padua \"Padua\"), [Caffè dell'Ussero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palazzo_Agostini,_Pisa \"Palazzo Agostini, Pisa\") in [Pisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pisa \"Pisa\"), and [Caffè Fiorio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffè_Fiorio \"Caffè Fiorio\") in [Turin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turin \"Turin\").\n\n#### Netherlands\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=15 \"Edit section: Netherlands\")]\n\nNot to be confused with retail outlets for [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis \"Cannabis\"), which are named [coffeeshop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffeeshop_(Netherlands) \"Coffeeshop (Netherlands)\") in the Netherlands.\n\nCoffeehouses (koffiehuizen) emerged in the [Dutch Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic \"Dutch Republic\") in the mid-17th century following the introduction of coffee through expanding trade networks with the [Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East \"Middle East\") and the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire \"Ottoman Empire\"). The earliest documented references to coffee-related establishments in the Netherlands date to the early 1660s, particularly in Amsterdam. One of the earliest known coffeehouses in the country is recorded in The Hague, where a coffeehouse reportedly opened on the Korte Voorhout in 1664. At this time, coffee was a luxury commodity, primarily consumed by wealthier groups, while traditional beverages such as beer remained dominant. By the late 17th century, coffeehouses had become established in major Dutch cities, including Amsterdam, where contemporary sources indicate the presence of multiple coffee sellers and dedicated establishments. These venues also functioned as social spaces for conversation, gaming, and the exchange of news and information. Coffeehouses in the Dutch Republic formed part of a broader European development in which such establishments contributed to urban sociability and the circulation of public discourse.[[80]](#cite_note-82)[[81]](#cite_note-83)[[82]](#cite_note-84)\n\n#### Portugal\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=16 \"Edit section: Portugal\")]\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| This section **does not [cite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources \"Wikipedia:Citing sources\") any [sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse \"Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse\") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners \"Help:Referencing for beginners\"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\"). *(January 2026)* *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Estatua_de_Fernando_Pessoa.jpg/250px-Estatua_de_Fernando_Pessoa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Estatua_de_Fernando_Pessoa.jpg)\n\n[Statue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue \"Statue\") of [Fernando Pessoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Pessoa \"Fernando Pessoa\") by Lagoa Henriques, next to the [*A Brasileira*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_A_Brasileira \"Café A Brasileira\") café, in [Chiado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiado \"Chiado\"), Lisbon\n\nThe history of coffee in [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal \"Portugal\") is usually said to have begun during the reign of king [John V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_V_of_Portugal \"John V of Portugal\"), when Portuguese agent Francisco de Melo Palheta supposedly managed to steal coffee beans from French Guiana and introduce it to Brazil.[[83]](#cite_note-Ukers1922-85) From Brazil, coffee was taken to [Cape Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Verde \"Cape Verde\") and [São Tomé and Príncipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Tomé_and_Príncipe \"São Tomé and Príncipe\"), which were also Portuguese colonies at the time. Despite this story, coffee already existed in [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angola \"Angola\"), having been introduced by [Portuguese missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionaries \"Missionaries\").[[84]](#cite_note-ClarenceSmith1985-86) During the 18th century, the first public *cafés* appeared, inspired by French gatherings[*[vague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Vagueness \"Wikipedia:Vagueness\")*] from the 17th century, becoming spaces for cultural and artistic entertainment.\n\nSeveral cafés emerged in [Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon \"Lisbon\") such as: *Martinho da Arcada* (the oldest café still operating, having opened in 1782), *Café Tavares*, and *Botequim Parras*. Of these, several became famous for harboring poets and artists, such as [Manuel du Bocage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Maria_Barbosa_du_Bocage \"Manuel Maria Barbosa du Bocage\") with his visits to *Café Nicola,* opened in 1796 by the Italian Nicola Breteiro; and [Fernando Pessoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Pessoa \"Fernando Pessoa\") with his visits to [*A Brasileira*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_A_Brasileira \"Café A Brasileira\"), opened in 1905 by Adriano Teles. The most famous was the *Café Marrare*, opened by the Neapolitan Antonio Marrare, in 1820, and frequently visited by Júlio Castilho, Raimundo de Bulhão Pato, [Almeida Garrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almeida_Garrett \"Almeida Garrett\"), [Alexandre Herculano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Herculano \"Alexandre Herculano\") and other members of the Portuguese government and the [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia \"Intelligentsia\"). It began its saying: *Lisboa era [Chiado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiado \"Chiado\"), o Chiado era o Marrare e o Marrare ditava a lei* (English: 'Lisbon was the Chiado, the Chiado was the Marrare and the Marrare dictated the law').\n\nOther coffeehouses soon opened across the country, such as *Café Vianna,* opened in [Braga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braga \"Braga\"), in 1858, by Manoel José da Costa Vianna, and visited by important Portuguese writers such as [Camilo Castelo Branco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camilo_Castelo_Branco \"Camilo Castelo Branco\") and [Eça de Queirós](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eça_de_Queiroz \"Eça de Queiroz\"). During the 1930s, a surge in coffeehouses happened in [Porto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto \"Porto\") with the opening of several that still exist, such as *Café Guarany*, opened in 1933, and *A Regaleira*, opened in 1934.\n\n#### Romania\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=17 \"Edit section: Romania\")]\n\nIn 1667, Kara Hamie, a former Ottoman [Janissary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissary \"Janissary\") from [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople \"Constantinople\"), opened the first coffee shop in the center of [Bucharest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bucharest \"Bucharest\") (then the capital of the [Principality of Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia \"Wallachia\")). On its site today stands the main building of the [National Bank of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_of_Romania \"National Bank of Romania\").[[85]](#cite_note-87)\n\n#### Switzerland\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=18 \"Edit section: Switzerland\")]\n\nIn 1761 the [Turm Kaffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turm_Kaffee?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Turm Kaffee (page does not exist)\") [[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turm%20Kaffee \"de:Turm Kaffee\")], a shop for exported goods, was opened in [St. Gallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gallen \"St. Gallen\").[[86]](#cite_note-88)[*[better source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")*]\n\n#### Gender\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=19 \"Edit section: Gender\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Interior_of_a_London_Coffee-house%2C_17th_centuryFXD.jpg/250px-Interior_of_a_London_Coffee-house%2C_17th_centuryFXD.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Interior_of_a_London_Coffee-house,_17th_centuryFXD.jpg)\n\nAn English café at the close of the 17th century: men hang their hats on pegs and sit at long communal tables strewn with papers and writing implements. Coffee pots are ranged at an open fire, with a hanging cauldron of boiling water. The only woman present is [separated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_segregation \"Sex segregation\") in a canopied booth, from which she serves coffee in tall cups.[[87]](#cite_note-89)\n\nThe exclusion of women from coffeehouses as guests was not universal, but does appear to have been common in Europe. In Germany, women frequented them, but in England and France they were banned in the mid-17th century.[[88]](#cite_note-90) [Émilie du Chtelet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Émilie_du_Chtelet \"Émilie du Chtelet\") reportedly [cross-dressed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-dressing \"Cross-dressing\") to gain entrance to the Café Gradot, in [Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris \"Paris\").[[89]](#cite_note-91)\n\nWomen did work as waitresses at coffeehouses and also owned and managed coffeehouses. Well-known women in the coffeehouse business were [Moll King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moll_King_(coffee_house_proprietor) \"Moll King (coffee house proprietor)\") in England[[90]](#cite_note-92) and [Maja-Lisa Borgman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maja-Lisa_Borgman \"Maja-Lisa Borgman\") in Sweden.[[91]](#cite_note-93)\n\n### The Americas\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=20 \"Edit section: The Americas\")]\n\n#### Argentina\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=21 \"Edit section: Argentina\")]\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| This section **does not [cite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources \"Wikipedia:Citing sources\") any [sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse \"Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse\") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners \"Help:Referencing for beginners\"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\"). *(January 2026)* *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Avenida_de_Mayo_Caf%C3%A9_Tortoni.jpg/250px-Avenida_de_Mayo_Caf%C3%A9_Tortoni.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Avenida_de_Mayo_Café_Tortoni.jpg)\n\n[Café Tortoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Tortoni \"Café Tortoni\") is a café in Buenos Aires frequented by [Jorge Luis Borges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Luis_Borges \"Jorge Luis Borges\") among other public figures.\n\nCoffeehouses are part of the culture of [Buenos Aires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buenos_Aires \"Buenos Aires\") and the customs of its inhabitants. They are traditional meeting places for *[porteños](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porteños \"Porteños\")* and have inspired innumerable artistic creations. Some notable coffeehouses include [Confitería del Molino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confitería_del_Molino \"Confitería del Molino\"), [Café Tortoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Tortoni \"Café Tortoni\"), [El Gato Negro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Gato_Negro_(café) \"El Gato Negro (café)\"), and [Café La Biela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_La_Biela \"Café La Biela\").\n\n#### United States\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=22 \"Edit section: United States\")]\n\nThe first coffeehouse in the United States opened in [Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston \"Boston\"), in 1676.[[92]](#cite_note-94)[*[better source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")*] However, Americans did not start choosing coffee over tea until the [Boston Tea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Tea_Party \"Boston Tea Party\") and the [Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War \"American Revolutionary War\"). After the Revolutionary War, Americans briefly went back to drinking tea until after the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812 \"War of 1812\") when they began importing high-quality coffee from Latin America and expensive inferior-quality tea from American shippers instead of Great Britain.[[93]](#cite_note-Wolf_WhatWeEat-95)[[94]](#cite_note-96) Whether they were drinking coffee or tea, coffeehouses, like those in Great Britain, were places where business was done. In the 1780s, Merchant's Coffee House on Wall Street in New York City was home to the organization of the [Bank of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_New_York_Mellon_v._Realogy_Corp. \"Bank of New York Mellon v. Realogy Corp.\") and the [New York Chamber of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Commerce_of_the_State_of_New_York \"Chamber of Commerce of the State of New York\").[[95]](#cite_note-97)\n\nCoffeehouses in the United States arose from the [espresso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espresso \"Espresso\")- and pastry-centered Italian coffeehouses of the Italian American immigrant communities in the major U.S. cities, notably [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City \"New York City\")'s [Little Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Italy,_Manhattan \"Little Italy, Manhattan\") and [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village \"Greenwich Village\"), Boston's [North End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_End,_Boston \"North End, Boston\"), and San Francisco's [North Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Beach,_San_Francisco \"North Beach, San Francisco\"). From the late 1950s onward, coffeehouses also hosted entertainment, most commonly [folk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music \"Folk music\") performers during the [American folk music revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_folk_music_revival \"American folk music revival\").[[8]](#cite_note-Groce_Coffeehouses-8)[[96]](#cite_note-98) Both Greenwich Village and North Beach became major haunts of the [Beats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_Generation \"Beat Generation\"), who were highly identified with these coffeehouses. As the youth culture of the 1960s evolved, non-Italians consciously copied these coffeehouses. The political nature of much of 1960s folk music made the music a natural tie-in with coffeehouses with their association with political action. A number of well-known performers like [Joan Baez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Baez \"Joan Baez\") and [Bob Dylan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dylan \"Bob Dylan\") began their careers performing in coffeehouses. [Blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues \"Blues\") singer [Lightnin' Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightnin'_Hopkins \"Lightnin' Hopkins\") bemoaned his woman's overindulgence in coffeehouse socializing in his 1969 song \"Coffeehouse Blues\".[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\nIn 1966, [Alfred Peet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Peet \"Alfred Peet\") began applying the dark roast style to high quality beans and opened up a small shop in [Berkeley, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkeley,_California \"Berkeley, California\") to educate customers on the virtues of good coffee.[[93]](#cite_note-Wolf_WhatWeEat-95) Starting in 1967 with the opening of the [Last Exit on Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Exit_on_Brooklyn \"Last Exit on Brooklyn\") coffeehouse, [Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle \"Seattle\") became known for its thriving [countercultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture \"Counterculture\") coffeehouse scene; the [Starbucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbucks \"Starbucks\") chain later standardized and mainstreamed this [espresso bar](#Espresso_bar) model, now prevalent throughout the country.[[97]](#cite_note-99)[[98]](#cite_note-100)[[99]](#cite_note-101)\n\nIn the 21st century, North American usage has increasingly distinguished between the terms \"coffee shop\" and \"café\" based on their operational models and revenue streams. While often used interchangeably in casual speech, a **coffee shop** is typically characterized by a focus on \"[extraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_extraction \"Coffee extraction\")\" (beverages), [counter service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter_service \"Counter service\"), and high-volume throughput. In contrast, a **café** often functions as a hybrid between a restaurant and a traditional coffeehouse, prioritizing seated dining and a higher average transaction value. This distinction is further defined by physical infrastructure; cafés often require full commercial kitchen licensing, including [grease traps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grease_trap \"Grease trap\") and high-capacity [ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventilation_(architecture) \"Ventilation (architecture)\") for meal preparation, whereas coffee shops frequently operate under \"light food\" licenses that prioritize [the espresso machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espresso_machine \"Espresso machine\") as the central focal point of the space.[[100]](#cite_note-102)\n\nFrom the 1960s through the mid-1980s, churches and individuals in the United States used the coffeehouse concept for outreach. They were often storefronts and had names like *The Lost Coin* ([Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village \"Greenwich Village\")), *The Gathering Place* ([Riverside, CA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverside,_California \"Riverside, California\")), *Catacomb Chapel* (New York City), and *Jesus for You* ([Buffalo, NY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo,_New_York \"Buffalo, New York\")). Christian music (often guitar-based) was performed, coffee and food provided, and [Bible studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_study_(Christianity) \"Bible study (Christianity)\") convened as people of varying backgrounds gathered in a casual setting that was purposefully different from traditional churches.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n* [![Caffe Reggio on MacDougal Street in Manhattan, founded in 1927](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Caffe_Reggio_01.jpg/250px-Caffe_Reggio_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Caffe_Reggio_01.jpg \"Caffe Reggio on MacDougal Street in Manhattan, founded in 1927\")\n\n  [Caffe Reggio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffe_Reggio \"Caffe Reggio\") on [MacDougal Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MacDougal_Street \"MacDougal Street\") in Manhattan, founded in 1927\n* [![A Starbucks coffee shop in Knoxville, Tennessee with a drive-through](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Exterior_of_a_modern_Starbucks_in_Knoxville%2C_Tennessee.jpg/250px-Exterior_of_a_modern_Starbucks_in_Knoxville%2C_Tennessee.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Exterior_of_a_modern_Starbucks_in_Knoxville,_Tennessee.jpg \"A Starbucks coffee shop in Knoxville, Tennessee with a drive-through\")\n\n  A Starbucks coffee shop in [Knoxville, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knoxville,_Tennessee \"Knoxville, Tennessee\") with a drive-through\n* [![Café Hagen, Seattle, Washington (state)](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Cafe_Hagen_at_1252_Thomas_Street%2C_South_Lake_Union%2C_Seattle_-_1.jpg/250px-Cafe_Hagen_at_1252_Thomas_Street%2C_South_Lake_Union%2C_Seattle_-_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cafe_Hagen_at_1252_Thomas_Street,_South_Lake_Union,_Seattle_-_1.jpg \"Café Hagen, Seattle, Washington (state)\")\n\n  [Café Hagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Hagen \"Café Hagen\"), [Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle \"Seattle\"), [Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_(state) \"Washington (state)\")\n\n## Contemporary history\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=23 \"Edit section: Contemporary history\")]\n\nSee also: [List of coffeehouse chains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_coffeehouse_chains \"List of coffeehouse chains\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Pastries_sold_at_a_coffee_shop.jpg/250px-Pastries_sold_at_a_coffee_shop.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pastries_sold_at_a_coffee_shop.jpg)\n\nCoffeehouses often sell [pastries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastries \"Pastries\") or other food items.\n\nA café may have an outdoor section (on a terrace, pavement or [sidewalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidewalk_cafe \"Sidewalk cafe\")) with seats, tables and parasols. This is especially common in Europe. Cafés offer a more open public space than many of the traditional pubs they have replaced, which were more male-dominated with a focus on alcohol.\n\nOne of the original uses of the café, as a place for information exchange and communication, was reintroduced in the 1990s with the [Internet café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_café \"Internet café\") or [hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotspot_(Wi-Fi) \"Hotspot (Wi-Fi)\").[[101]](#cite_note-103)[*[better source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")*] The spread of modern-style cafés to urban and rural areas went hand-in-hand with the rising use of mobile computers. Computers and Internet access and contemporary decor help to create a youthful, modern place, compared to the traditional pubs or old-fashioned [diners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diner \"Diner\") that they replaced.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n### Africa\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=24 \"Edit section: Africa\")]\n\n#### Egypt\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=25 \"Edit section: Egypt\")]\n\nCoffeehouses in Egypt are colloquially called *ʿahwah* /ʔhwa/, the dialectal pronunciation of قَهْوة *qahwah* (literally \"coffee\")[[c]](#cite_note-105) These were named after what was then the most popular drink that they served,[[103]](#cite_note-Gezim-106) though also commonly served in *ʿahwah* are tea (*shāy*) and [herbal teas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbal_tea \"Herbal tea\"), especially the highly popular [hibiscus blend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hibiscus_tea \"Hibiscus tea\") ([Egyptian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Arabic \"Egyptian Arabic\"): *karkadeh* or *ennab*).\n\nThe first *ʿahwah* opened around the 1850s but for a long time were patronized mostly by older people, with youths frequenting but not always ordering. However:\n\n> By the 1920s, there were many coffee shops throughout Egypt. They could be classified into three major kinds: the [Alexandrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria \"Alexandria\") *bursa*, the [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo \"Cairo\") club, and the rural *gharza* (inn). ... [All three] gradually became significant centres where Egyptians sipped the spirit of integration and unity.[[103]](#cite_note-Gezim-106)\n\n#### Ethiopia\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=26 \"Edit section: Ethiopia\")]\n\nIn [Addis Ababa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addis_Ababa \"Addis Ababa\"), the capital of Ethiopia, independent coffeehouses that struggled before 1991 have become popular with young professionals who do not have time for traditional coffee roasting at home.[[d]](#cite_note-107) One that has become well known is [Tomoca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomoca_Coffee \"Tomoca Coffee\"), which opened in 1953.[[104]](#cite_note-108)\n\n### Asia\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=27 \"Edit section: Asia\")]\n\n#### India\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=28 \"Edit section: India\")]\n\nIn India, coffee culture has expanded in the past twenty years. Chains like [Indian Coffee House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Coffee_House \"Indian Coffee House\"), [Café Coffee Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_Coffee_Day \"Café Coffee Day\"), and [Barista Lavazza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barista_Lavazza \"Barista Lavazza\") have become very popular.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] Cafés are considered good places to conduct office meetings and for friends to meet.[[105]](#cite_note-109)\n\n#### China\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=29 \"Edit section: China\")]\n\nIn China, a growing number of domestic coffeehouse chains have emerged since the 1990s. Among them, [Luckin Coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luckin_Coffee \"Luckin Coffee\"), founded in Beijing in 2017, has become a notable homegrown challenger to Western brands that have dominated China's market such as [Starbucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbucks \"Starbucks\"). By leveraging a digital-first, app-based model and aggressive pricing, Luckin rapidly expanded to over 20,000 stores in China by 2024, overtaking Starbucks as the country's largest coffee chain by revenue and store count. Starbucks' market share in China fell from a peak of 42% in 2017 to 14% in 2024, while Luckin posted strong revenue and profit growth. In 2025, Luckin took its rivalry global by opening its first U.S. stores in [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City \"New York City\"), signaling the rise of a domestic Chinese coffee brand on the world stage. Coffee culture continues to expand in major cities, though tea remains the dominant and historical daily beverage for most of the population.[[106]](#cite_note-LuckinCampaign-110)[[107]](#cite_note-LuckinFoodTalks-111)[[108]](#cite_note-LuckinYicai-112)[[109]](#cite_note-LuckinInvestor-113)\n\n#### Malaysia and Singapore\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=30 \"Edit section: Malaysia and Singapore\")]\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| This section **does not [cite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources \"Wikipedia:Citing sources\") any [sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse \"Special:EditPage/Coffeehouse\") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners \"Help:Referencing for beginners\"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\"). *(January 2026)* *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |\n\nIn Malaysia and Singapore, traditional breakfast and coffee shops are called *[kopi tiam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopi_tiam \"Kopi tiam\")*. The word is a [compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compound_(linguistics) \"Compound (linguistics)\") of the [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language \"Malay language\") word for coffee (as borrowed and altered from English) and the [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Min_Nan \"Min Nan\") dialect word for shop (店; [POJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pe̍h-ōe-jī \"Pe̍h-ōe-jī\"): tiàm). Menus typically offer a variety of simple dishes based on [egg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eggs_as_food \"Eggs as food\"), [toast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toast_(food) \"Toast (food)\"), and [coconut jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut_jam \"Coconut jam\"), plus coffee, tea, and [Milo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milo_(drink) \"Milo (drink)\"), a popular malted chocolate drink.\n\n#### Indonesia\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=31 \"Edit section: Indonesia\")]\n\nIn Indonesia, traditional coffeehouses are called *kedai kopi*, *rumah kopi*, or *warung kopi* which is often abbreviated as *warkop*. *[Kopi tubruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopi_tubruk \"Kopi tubruk\")* (resembling [Turkish coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_coffee \"Turkish coffee\")) is a common drink in small *warkop*. To accompany this, traditional *[kue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kue \"Kue\")* is also served. The first coffeehouse in Indonesia was founded in 1878 in [Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta \"Jakarta\") and named *Warung Tinggi Tek Sun Ho*.[[110]](#cite_note-114)\n\n#### Philippines\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=32 \"Edit section: Philippines\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/e/ec/Coffee_Overdose_Stand.jpg/250px-Coffee_Overdose_Stand.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Coffee_Overdose_Stand.jpg)\n\nA coffeehouse stand in [Angeles City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angeles_City \"Angeles City\"), [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines \"Philippines\")\n\nIn the Philippines, coffee shop chains like [Starbucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbucks \"Starbucks\") have become the prevalent hangouts for upper- and middle-class professionals in such districts as the [Makati CBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati_Central_Business_District \"Makati Central Business District\"). However, *carinderias* (small eateries) continue to serve coffee alongside breakfast and snack dishes. Events called *kapihan* (fora) are often held inside bakeshops or restaurants that also serve coffee for breakfast or [merienda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merienda \"Merienda\").[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] A number of places often called \"cafés\" serve not just coffee and pastries but full meals, often international cuisine adapted to Filipino tastes.[[111]](#cite_note-115)\n\n#### Thailand\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Coffeehouse&action=edit§ion=33 \"Edit section: Thailand\")]\n\nIn Thailand, the term \"café\" not only is a coffeehouse as understood elsewhere, but in the past was considered a [bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_(establishment) \"Bar (establishment)\") serving [alcoholic drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_drink \"Alcoholic drink\") during a comedy show on stage. This type of business flourished in the 1990s, before the [1997 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis \"1997 Asian financial crisis\").[[112]](#cite_note-116)\n\nThe first real coffeehouse in Thailand opened in 1917 at the [Si Kak Phraya Si](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Si_Kak_Phraya_Si \"Si Kak Phraya Si\") in the area of [Rattanakosin Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Island \"Rattanakosin Island\"), by [Madam Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edna_Sarah_Cole \"Edna Sarah Cole\"), an American woman then living in Thailand. Later, [Chao Phraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phraya \"Phraya\") Ram Rakop (เจ้าพระยารามราฆพ), a Thai aristocrat, opened a coffeehouse named \"Café de Norasingha\" (คาเฟ่นรสิงห์) at Sanam Suea Pa (สนามเสือป่า), next to the [Royal Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Plaza_(Thailand) \"Royal Plaza (Thailand)\").[[113]](#cite_note-117) Café de Norasingha has been renovated and moved within [Phayathai Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phayathai_Palace \"Phayathai Palace\").[[114]](#cite_note-118) In the [southern region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Thailand \"Southern Thailand\"), a traditional coffeehouse or *kopi tiam* is popular with locals, like many countries in the [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula \"Malay Peninsula\").[*[dubious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement \"Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute\") – [discuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Coffeehouse#Dubious \"Talk:Coffeehouse\")*][[115]](#cite_note-119)\n\n* [![A coffee shop in Bacoor, Philippines](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Caffeena_caf%C3%A9_storefront_at_NOMO%2C_Bacoor%2C_Cavite_%E2%80%94_25_Feb_2022.jpg/250px-Caffeena_caf%C3%A9_storefront_at_NOMO%2C_Bacoor%2C_Cavite_%E2%80%94_25_Feb_2022.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Caffeena_café_storefront_at_NOMO,_Bacoor,_Cavite_—_25_Feb_2022.jpg \"A coffee shop in Bacoor, Philippines\")\n\n  A coffee shop in [Bacoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacoor \"Bacoor\"), Philippines\n* [![Rumah Loer, a contemporary-style coffee shop (Indonesian: rumah kopi kekinian) in Palembang, ([Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language \"Indonesian language\"): *rumah kopi kekinian*) in [Palembang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palembang \"Palembang\"),
